 SUMMARY OF TESTING

 A) Objectives

 The principal objectives of the DM100 tests were to determine the effects of glass
property extremes (glass viscosity, electrical conductivity, and T1%), minimum and maximum
concentrations of several major and minor glass components, +/-15% batching errors in the
addition of Glass Forming Chemicals (GFCs) to the feed, and variation in the sources of boron
and sodium used in the GFCs on processing rates and melter conditions (off-gas characteristics,
glass processing, foaming, cold cap, etc.) at constant bubbling rate. Samples from these tests
were used to assess the effects on feed rheology and glass durability as a result of these changes.
Completion of the test objectives is detailed in the following table:

 B) Test Exceptions

 None

 C) Results and Performance Against the Objectives

 Seven tests using different High Level Waste (HLW) feeds were conducted on the
DM100-BL to determine the effect of variations in glass properties and feed composition on
processing rates and melter conditions (off-gas characteristics, glass processing, foaming, cold
cap, etc.) at constant bubbling rate (9 lpm). In over seven hundred hours of testing, the property
extremes of glass viscosity, electrical conductivity, and T1%, as well as minimum and maximum
concentrations of several major and minor glass components were evaluated using glass
compositions that have been tested previously at the crucible scale. Other parameters evaluated
with respect to glass processing properties were +/-15% batching errors in the addition of GFCs
to the feed, and variation in the sources of boron and sodium used in the GFCs. Tests evaluating
batching errors and GFC source employed variations on the HLW98-86 formulation (a glass

 12

composition formulated for HLW C-106/AY-102 waste and processed in several previous melter
tests) in order to best isolate the effect of each test variable. Steady state processing rates were
achieved for each composition without significant problems except for tests with the most
viscous and least conductive glass formulations, which required sugar additions near the test mid
points in order to reduce excessive foaming that severely limited feed processing rates. The
steady-state production rates ranged between 900 to 1600 kg/m2/day, bracketed the range of
steady-state production rates (1100 to 1400 kg/m2/day) measured on other WTP HLW waste
streams processed on the DM100 under similar conditions, and were higher for lower viscosity
(and higher electrical conductivity) glass compositions. The sugar additions during tests with
viscous glass formulations allowed a near doubling of production rates from about 500 to 900
kg/m2/day. The glass processing difficulties from foaming occurred with the more viscous melts,
presumably due to the inability of gas to readily escape from the melt. Foaming did not increase
with glass manganese content and, in fact, the glass with the highest manganese concentration
processed at the highest rate of any the compositions tested, with no foaming problems. Tests
processing the HLW98-86 formulation with fifteen percent GFC batching errors and with boric
acid and soda ash replacing borax all resulted in steady-state production rates of between 1000
and 1050 kg/m2/day, indicating that these changes do not have a discernable effect on processing
rates.

 Samples from the melter feed, glass pool, and product glass were collected and analyzed
throughout testing to meet a variety of data objectives. Dip samples collected from the melt pool
at the end of each test and discharge glass samples indicated that no separate phases had formed
on the melt surface or in the poured glass. Glass discharge samples were analyzed for
composition and other properties for comparison to target properties. Most compositional
analysis results and physical property (viscosity and electrical conductivity) measurements
approximate the target for each test. Glass samples from tests with sugar additions to the feed
were analyzed for divalent iron concentration to determine its effect on glass oxidation state;
measured values from different compositions show different responses to the same level of sugar
addition. A series of glass samples from the melt pool, discharge, and melter bottom (sampling
boat) from during and after a 1000 hour idling period were analyzed to determine the extent of
crystallization at glass temperatures of about 950°C. Analysis of these samples demonstrated the
extent of crystallization near the melt pool floor; however, additional idling tests with sampling
boats at various locations would be useful to differentiate between the effects of settling and
enhanced crystal formation at the lower temperatures near the melter floor. Glass product
samples from tests with high concentrations of toxic metals were evaluated for chemical
durability using the Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP) and shown to be well
within regulatory limits.

 Total particulate carry-over from the melter was 0.3 to 3.37 percent; however, results
from five of the eight samples were within the narrow range of 0.63 to 0.89 percent of feed
solids. This narrow range of solids carryover is well within the range previously measured on the
DM100 when processing HLW simulants. The three sample results outside this range are
probably attributable to transient changes in the cold cap since the operational parameters were
kept constant during each test and the compositional variations are unlikely to result in such
large changes. Melter DFs were determined for most elements in the feed for all tests performed.
The most volatile species were halides, sulfur, selenium, and thallium and to a lesser degree

 13

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

boron, chromium, and arsenic. Gaseous emissions of nitrogen oxides and byproducts of
incomplete combustion, such as carbon monoxide and ammonia, were very low due to the lack
of nitrates and organic carbon in the feed. In tests with sugar added to the feed, higher
concentrations of these byproducts were observed, as also were lower concentrations of nitrogen
oxide.

 D) Quality Requirements

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program based on Nuclear Quality
Assurance (NQA)-1 (1989) and NQA-2a (1990) Part 2.7 that is in place at the VSL. This
program is supplemented by a VSL Quality Assurance Project Plan (QAPP) for RPP-WTP work.
Test and procedure requirements by which the testing activities are planned and controlled are
also defined in this plan. The program is supported by VSL standard operating procedures that
were used for this work. This work was not subject to DOE/RW-0333P or the WTP Quality
Assurance Project Plan for Testing Programs Generating Environmental Regulatory Data (QAPjP) for
environmental regulatory data.

 E) R&T Test Conditions

 The melter tests were conducted using the DM100-BL melter, which has a melt surface
area of 0.108 m2. The DM100-BL is a continuously fed, ceramic refractory-lined melter
equipped with a lance bubbler for glass pool agitation. The glass-melt is joule-heated using two
pairs of opposing Inconel electrodes. Feed was introduced into the melter with a peristaltic pump
to provide uniform feed delivery into the melter. For operational simplicity the melter is
equipped with a dry off-gas treatment system involving gas filtration operations only.

 Seven, nominally 100-hour tests with different HLW feeds were conducted on the
DM100-BL to determine processing rates and off-gas characteristics at constant bubbling rate
(nominally, 9 lpm). Production rates, feed distribution across molten glass, and off-gas
characteristics were determined for each feed. Dip samples were collected from the melt pool at
the end of each test; these samples and discharge glass samples were tested to determine whether
separate phases had formed on the melt surface or in the poured glass. Glass samples were
analyzed for composition to calculate elemental mass balances. Glass samples from tests with
sugar additions to the feed were also analyzed for their divalent iron concentration to determine
the extent of reduction in the glass. Glass produced from feed high in toxic metals was evaluated
for chemical durability using the TCLP.

 A nearly 1000-hour idling period at a melt temperature of 950°C was used to determine
the extent of crystallization for a glass with a low T1% value. Glass pool and discharge samples
from before, during, and after the idling period were taken and analyzed for crystal content. In
addition, a sampling boat was fabricated and installed on the melter floor during the idling
period; samples recovered from the boat were then analyzed for crystal content.

 14

 Melter emissions data were collected during the steady-state portions of each test. The
melter exhaust was sampled for metals/particles according to 40-CFR-60 Methods 3, 5, and 29.
Particulate collection required isokinetic sampling. Melter emissions were monitored for a
variety of gaseous components, notably CO and nitrogen species, by Fourier Transform
Infra-Red Spectroscopy (FTIR). Feed samples and melter glass samples (both discharged glass
samples and dip samples) were analyzed for their chemical composition. Rheological properties
of the feed samples were also measured.

 F) Simulant Use

 This testing used the HLW C-106/AY-102 simulant with a composition described in
Section 2.0; this composition was defined in a BNI Test Specification (24590-HLW-TSP-RT-02-
005).

 The HLW matrix glasses (HLW02-26 and HLW02-46) specified for use in these tests
were (statistically) formulated to cover optimally the glass composition regions of interest and
were not individually based on any specific waste composition. The waste simulants and melter
feeds were formulated assuming component oxides. Based on waste and melter feed
formulations employed in earlier melter testing, it was assumed that all glass oxides result from
the waste, with the exceptions of B2O3, Li2O, Na2O, SiO2, and ZnO which are glass forming
additives. Other assumptions about the waste, such as volatile concentrations and solids content,
were the same as those used for the HLW C-106/AY-102 simulant.

 The waste compositions are the starting points of algorithm glass calculations for HLW-
ALG-16 and HLW-ALG-17. Formulation of the waste simulants for the calculated algorithm
glasses, therefore, makes use of the pre-defined waste compositions and requires no extra
assumptions. The IHLW algorithm results also include specification of the glass former mix.
Other assumptions about the waste, such as volatile concentrations and solids content, were the
same as those used for the HLW C-106/AY-102 simulant.

 G) Issues and Follow-On Tests

 The results of these tests suggest that foaming in the melt pool, which severely limits feed
processing rates, depends on the physical properties of the glass melt, increasing with increasing
viscosity. The concentration of manganese did not appear to be a significant factor. Additions of
sugar were demonstrated to be effective in recovering manageable processing conditions;
however, the effects of sugar additions on the oxidation state of the glass varied significantly
with feed composition. These results highlight the need for the development of protocols for the
a priori determination of which HLW feeds will require sugar additions and the appropriate
amounts of sugar to be added in order to control foaming and maintain throughput without
over-reduction of the melt (which could lead to molten metal formation).

 Testing conducted on feed samples demonstrated that variations in the additive to waste
ratio and in the additive type have the potential to create a significantly more viscous feed.

 15

Additional testing is required to better understand the effect of additive source and waste to
additive ratio on feed rheology.

 A series of glass samples from the melt pool, discharge, and melter bottom (sampling
boat) from during and after a 1000 hour idling period were analyzed to determine the extent of
crystallization at glass temperatures of about 950°C. Analysis of these samples demonstrated the
extent of crystallization near the melt pool floor; however, additional idling tests with sampling
boats at various locations would be useful to differentiate between the effects of settling and
enhanced crystal formation at the lower temperatures near the melter floor.

 Feed sample analyses from the present tests highlighted the potential for glass forming
chemicals (GFCs) to introduce larger than desired contaminant levels of various oxides, and
specifically aluminum in the present case. Such events have been rare over the long history of
similar purchases from the same vendor but suggest the need for confirmatory analysis of the
source chemicals used at the WTP. WTP GFC procurement requirements will specify maximum
contaminant levels but may need to include more extensive mineral source batch analyses than
currently planned. The extent and source of contamination can only be determined by analysis of
the source chemicals.

 It is noted that the Test Specification and Test Plan for this work include a series of tests
to address the effect of elemental compositional variations on glass production rates and melter
emissions; these tests have not been completed at the time of writing. Also covered in these
documents are tests on the DM10 to determine the maximum concentration of sulfur that can be
accommodated in two HLW glass compositions without forming secondary sulfur phases.
Results from these tests will be reported separately and compared to the results presented in the
present report.

 16

 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

 Acceptable glass formulations for vitrification of High Level Waste (HLW) streams at
the Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP) must meet a number of
product quality, processability, and waste loading requirements. Glass formulation development
and testing has been ongoing at the Vitreous State Laboratory (VSL) to address these
requirements, supporting the WTP Project in various areas including melter testing, actual waste
testing, and property-composition model development. In general, when the objective was to
support melter runs or actual waste testing, glass formulations were actively designed in order to
focus on the particular waste compositions that had been defined by the WTP Project. By
contrast, when the objective was to support development of property-composition models, glass
formulations were statistically designed in order to optimally cover the compositional regions of
interest, once these had been defined. This approach provides for more even and complete
coverage of the compositional regions under study and the resulting data are better suited to
property-composition modeling. Regardless of the design approach, however, testing of the glass
formulations began with fabrication of the glasses on crucible scale. The prepared glass was then
analyzed for composition before characterization with respect to the various product and
processability requirements. In the active-design approach, iterations of this testing process were
made if the glass formulated did not meet the various constraints, with the collected data fed
back for use in the formulation of the next set of glasses.

 Current WTP flow-sheet models and projections predict that the HLW feed compositions
delivered to the HLW vitrification facility will change continuously, even though initially only
four major waste groups are involved. In addition, the processing schedule will not allow
sufficient time to formulate glass compositions using an active-design and testing-based process.
Acceptable HLW glass compositions instead will be calculated for each batch of waste
transferred to the HLW vitrification facility. The algorithm for calculating these Immobilized
High Level Waste (IHLW) glass compositions is being developed at the WTP [1]. The initial
IHLW formulation algorithm employs constraints that are designed to address the various
product quality, processability, and waste loading requirements. It also employs preliminary
HLW property-composition models developed from previous HLW glass formulation testing to
predict various properties of the glasses. Verification and validation of the IHLW formulation
algorithm is needed to identify potential deficiencies and areas for possible improvement before
its implementation at the WTP.

 VSL has been developing and testing glass formulations for WTP waste envelopes to
provide data to meet contract requirements and to support system design activities [2-7]. That
work was based upon small-scale batch melts (“crucible melts”) using waste simulants. Selected
formulations have also been tested in small-scale continuously-fed joule-heated melters
(DM100) [8-12] and in the HLW Pilot Melter (DM1200) [11-19]. Such melter tests provide
information on key process factors such as feed processing behavior, dynamic effects during
processing, processing rates, off-gas amounts and compositions, foaming control, etc., that

 17

cannot be reliably obtained from crucible melts. All of this melter testing has been performed
with projected pretreated feed compositions, with adjustments to account for recycle streams,
blended with glass forming additives to target glass compositions well within the boundaries of
the IHLW formulation algorithm or Qualified Glass Composition Region (QGCR). The QGCR
boundaries attempt to cover the expected range of HLW compositions, but the boundaries are
often further constrained by the other product requirements. In order to establish the robustness
of the HLW vitrification process with respect to supporting a 3 MT/d per melter WTP mission
(potentially to be increased to 3.75 MT/d), it is necessary to determine whether the desired
melter throughputs can be achieved across the range of possible feed compositions, as defined by
both the dynamic process modeling and the QGCR. Additionally, undesirable secondary phases
could accumulate on the glass surface and lead to a number of deleterious consequences,
including the accelerated corrosion of melter components. Secondary phases can also form as
crystals that settle to the floor of the melt pool and eventually clog the glass discharge or form a
conductive path that can compromise joule heating of the glass pool. Consequently, for bounding
glass compositions to be processed at WTP, it is also important to identify glass composition and
property extremes that are prone to the formation of secondary phases.

 This report provides results for the first matrix of DM100 melter tests that were designed
to address the above needs, as detailed in the Test Plan for this work [20]. The first matrix
addresses bounding glass properties with previously formulated algorithm and matrix glasses [4,
5], as well as the effects of Glass Forming Chemical (GFC) type and batching errors [21] using
the previously processed HLW98-86 composition [10, 11, 17, 19]. The tests employ feed and
glass compositions that target processing property extremes and several chemical composition
extremes in order to determine their effects on glass production rate, processing characteristics,
and the tendency to form secondary phases. A subsequent report will be issued to present the
results from the tests in the second matrix described in the Test Plan, which investigates the
effect of compositional maxima and minima.

1.1 Test Objectives

 As listed in the Test Specification for this work [22], the principal objectives of these
tests are identified below. Section 6 of the Test Specification ("Test Matrix") describes test
variables in the following six areas:

 (1) Evaluate the impact of compositions and transitions on throughput
 (2) Assess T1% criterion in terms of crystal accumulation in the melter
 (3) Assess impact of high and low viscosity glass on melt rate for HLW
 (4) Assess impact of electrical conductivity on processing
 (5) Assess impact of waste loading or ±15% GFCs variability on processability
 (6) Assess impact of different GFC sources (borax and Na2CO3) for B2O3 and Na2O on
 melt stability and production rate

 The objectives of the Test Plan [20] for the tests described in this report, as listed in the
Test Specification for this work [22], are:

 18

 • Define melter testing matrices that provide sufficient coverage of the testing variables
 defined in Section 6, Test Conditions. The test matrix is to be provided in the Test Plan
 that defines each test variable and is to include a discussion of its development and bases
 of the testing strategy and approach. The order in which the variables are tested should be
 optimized, and the predecessor activities such as glass formulation support and the
 maturity of the QGCR boundary definitions should be considered.

 • Define the laboratory and small melter (DM10 and DM100) testing that is required for
 each variable defined in Section 6, Test Conditions. The maximum melter scale proposed
 should be based on the data quality requirements and the scale necessary to achieve that
 quality of data.

 • Safely perform DM10 and DM100 melter testing. The duration of each campaign or test
 period shall be sufficient to satisfy the objectives defined in the Test Plan.

 • For melter testing that seeks to find limits on secondary salt phase formation, sampling
 will be performed to differentiate between transient salt phases and a persistent and
 accumulating salt phase.

 • For melter testing at the T1% boundary, characterize crystal formation and accumulation
 in the melter during both production testing and idling.

 • For each test, establish baseline melter conditions so that throughput rates at steady state
 are measured as functions of feed composition and concentration. Results should be
 comparable to previous testing as much as possible.

 • Characterize the DM100 melter emissions (particulate, aerosol, and gaseous) under
 nominal steady-state operating conditions for inorganic and organic compounds.
 Emissions data are not for regulatory purposes, but for comparison to other DM100 tests.
 Quantify and document the occurrence and associated operating conditions of any melter
 off-gas volume surging events.

 • Obtain the necessary process measurements to provide mass and energy balances
 throughout the DM100 system, including process monitoring of power, voltage, current,
 resistance, temperatures, pressures, flow rates, and cooling water and air flows and inlet
 and outlet temperatures.

 • Obtain key glass property data (e.g., Product Consistency Test [PCT] response and
 Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure [TCLP] response) for selected glasses
 produced at melter scale. These data will be for comparison purposes and not subject to
 regulatory quality control/assurance requirements.

 The present report presents results from only the first matrix of tests, which constitutes
about half of the tests described in the Test Plan [20]; consequently, not all of these objectives
are addressed by the tests discussed in the present report. The balance of the tests will address

 19

compositional maxima and minima and salt formation, the results of which will be covered in a
subsequent report.

1.2 Quality Assurance

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program based on Nuclear Quality
Assurance (NQA)-1 (1989) and NQA-2a (1990) Part 2.7 that is in place at the VSL. This
program is supplemented by a VSL Quality Assurance Project Plan (QAPP) for RPP-WTP work
[23]. Test and procedure requirements by which the testing activities are planned and controlled
are also defined in this plan. The program is supported by VSL standard operating procedures
(SOPs) that were used for this work [24].

 This work did not generate data to support waste form qualification activities; nor did it
generate data to support environmental regulatory data to support permitting activities.
Therefore, this work is not subject to DOE/RW-0333P or the WTP Quality Assurance Project
Plan for Testing Programs Generating Environmental Regulatory Data (QAPjP) [25].

1.3 Experimental Procedures

 1.3.1 Crucible Melt Glass Batching and Preparation

 All selected HLW glasses fabricated at the crucible scale prior to melter testing were
prepared at VSL using reagent grade chemicals and VSL SOPs [24]. The following briefly
summarizes the procedural steps.

 Glass preparation begins with a batching sheet that provides information on the required
starting materials. The information includes the chemicals needed, identification of the chemicals
according to the vendors and catalog numbers, the associated purity, together with the quantity
required to produce a given amount of glass. The quantities of chemicals required are adjusted
based on the reagent purities. Chemicals are weighed and batched according to the batching
sheets.

 After the starting materials are weighed and batched, a blender is used to mix and
homogenize the starting materials before they are loaded into platinum/gold crucibles that are
engraved with individual identification numbers. The loaded platinum/gold crucible is placed
inside a Deltech DT-28 (or DT-29) furnace, the heating of which is controlled by a Eurotherm
2404 temperature controller. The melting temperature is 1150°C, at which the melt is kept for 2
hours. Mixing of the melt is accomplished mechanically using a platinum stirrer, beginning 20
minutes after the furnace temperature reaches 1150°C and continuing for the next 90 minutes.
The molten glass is poured at the end of 120 minutes onto a graphite plate to cool before
recovery.

 20

 1.3.2 Glass Analysis Procedures

 1.3.2.1 Composition

 Compositions of the HLW glasses were analyzed using X-ray Fluorescence Spectroscopy
(XRF) in accordance with VSL SOPs [24]. Powdered glass samples (−200 mesh) are analyzed
with an ARL 9400 wavelength dispersive XRF spectrometer, which is calibrated over a range of
glass compositions using standard reference materials traceable to the National Institute of
Standards and Technology (NIST), as well as waste glasses including the Argonne National
Laboratory – Low Activity Waste Reference Material (ANL-LRM), the Defense Waste
Processing Facility – Environmental Assessment (DWPF-EA) glass, and WTP HLW and Low
Activity Waste (LAW) glasses.

                   1.3.2.2 Viscosity

 The viscosity of the glass melt, η, was measured using a Brookfield viscometer per VSL
SOPs [24]. The viscosity is determined from the relation between torque and rotation speed of
the spindle in the glass melt. Measurements are normally performed in the temperature range of
950ºC to 1250ºC and the data are interpolated to standard temperatures using the Vogel-Fulcher
equation:

                                       ln η = [A/(T − To)] + C ,

where A, C, and To are fitting parameters. The equipment is calibrated at room temperature using
standard oils of known viscosity and then checked from 950ºC to 1250ºC using a NIST standard
reference glass (SRM 711). Both precision and accuracy of the viscosity measurement are
estimated to be within ± 15 relative%.

 1.3.2.3 Electrical Conductivity

 The electrical conductivity, σ, was determined, according to VSL SOPs [24], by
measuring the impedance of the glass melt as a function of frequency using a calibrated
platinum/rhodium probe attached to a Hewlett-Packard model 4194A impedance analyzer.
Measurements are performed over temperature ranges similar to those employed for the viscosity
measurements (950ºC to 1250ºC). The resulting frequency dependent impedance data are
analyzed in terms of an equivalent circuit to obtain the direct current conductivity. The measured
data are then interpolated to standard temperatures using the Vogel-Fulcher equation:

                                       ln σ = [A/(T − To)] + C,

where A, C, and To are fitting parameters. Estimated uncertainties in the conductivity
measurements are ± 20 relative%.

 21

 1.3.2.4 Product Consistency Test

 The PCT data for select HLW glasses were collected at VSL from tests performed at
90°C for 7 days according to VSL SOPs [24] that are compliant with American Society for
Testing and Materials (ASTM) C1285 [26], as required in Specification 1 of the WTP contract
[27]. Samples of crushed glass (4 g, 100-200 mesh, or 75-149 µm) are placed in 40 ml of test
solution (de-ionized water) inside 304L stainless steel vessels. All tests are conducted in
triplicate and in parallel with the DWPF-EA standard glass included in each test set. The
leachates are sampled after 7 days, when 1 ml of sampled leachate is mixed with 20 ml of 1M
HNO3 and the resulting solution analyzed by Direct Current Plasma - Atomic Emission
Spectroscopy (DCP-AES). Another 3 ml of the sampled leachate is used for pH measurement.

 Specification 1 of the WTP contract requires that the normalized PCT mass losses of B,
Na, and Li be below the respective values for the DWPF-EA glass. The nominal values for
normalized leachate concentrations from the DWPF-EA glass are 16.695, 13.346, and 9.565 g/l
for B, Na, and Li, respectively [28]. The corresponding value for Si is 3.922 g/l.

 1.3.2.5 Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP)

 TCLP was performed on glass samples per VSL SOPs [24] based on EPA TCLP leach
test (SW-846 Method 1311) that is used to determine the leach resistance of crushed glass (<
3/8”) in a sodium acetate buffer solution after 18 hours at 22°C with constant end-over-end
agitation. A mass of 100 grams of glass is leached in 2 liters of TCLP extract, according to the
extraction method for non-volatiles. The surface area to volume ratio for this test is about 20 m-1,
which is about two orders of magnitude lower than that in the PCT. The leachate concentrations
are measured by DCP-AES. The overall uncertainty associated with this test for glass samples is
estimated from evaluation of VSL data to be ± 20%.

 1.3.2.6 Determination of One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%)

 One-percent crystal fraction temperature (T1%) was determined per VSL SOPs [24] as
described below. Glass samples (about 5 grams each) were heat-treated in platinum, platinum-
gold, or platinum-rhodium crucibles (5 ml) at a pre-melt temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour,
followed by heat treatment for 70 hours at prescribed temperatures between 650°C and 1200°C.
At the end of the heat-treatment period, the glass samples are quenched by contacting the
crucible with cold water. This quenching freezes in the phase assemblage in equilibrium with the
melt at the heat-treatment temperature. The sample is then prepared for Scanning Electron
Microscopy/Energy Dispersive X-ray Spectroscopy (SEM/EDS) examination by grinding and
sieving (−18 mesh). The microscopic and spectroscopic examination (Model JSM-5910LV,
equipped with Oxford Instruments INCAEnergy 300 system) was used to determine the volume
fraction of crystalline phases and identify the dominant crystalline phases. For each glass, heat
treatments are performed to obtain non-zero vol% crystal data for at least three temperatures in

 22

order to reasonably constrain the T1% value. Efforts are also made to bracket the T1% temperature
so that it can be obtained by interpolation rather than extrapolation.

 The T1% value for each glass is obtained by linear regression of the heat-treatment
temperature (°C) as the dependent variable versus crystal fraction (vol%) as the independent
variable. The choice of vol% (which has the larger measurement error) as the independent
variable, rather than the temperature (which has the smaller measurement error), is contrary to
the selection that would normally be made for regression. However, as discussed in a previous
T1% modeling report [5], there are significant advantages to using this “inverse regression”
approach in the present application. The differences in the T1% values estimated using either
choice of independent variable are small. Based on results from modeling studies, the standard
deviation in estimating T1% values is about 27°C [5].

 1.3.2.7 Refractory Corrosion

 Corrosion tests on Monofrax™ K-3 refractory are performed per VSL SOPs [24] using a
modified version of the ASTM C-621-84 procedure. The test method involves sample coupon
preparation, coupon corrosion under controlled conditions, and characterization of the reacted
sample.

 A monolithic K-3 test coupon of known dimensions is cemented to a crucible cover made
of Zirmul and then positioned in a 200-ml platinum crucible containing about 170 g of
pre-melted glass. The K-3 coupon and the crucible are placed inside a quartz crucible holder,
which in turn is placed inside a furnace pre-heated to about 800°C. After the furnace reaches the
test temperature (normally 1208°C), a platinum bubbling tube is introduced into the molten glass
through a slot in the Zirmul cover. Dry air is bubbled at a constant rate of 8 ml/min. Each
corrosion test lasts six days with continuous air bubbling at 1208°C. All tests are performed at
the same refractory surface area (S)/melt volume (V) of about 0.20 cm-1, which is 74% less than
the S/V ratio specified by ASTM C-621-84. At the end of the test, the K-3 coupon is removed
from the melt and cooled to room temperature in a quartz crucible.

 Characterization of the reacted K-3 coupon begins with sectioning of the coupon
lengthwise at the midpoint. The thickness of the reacted K-3 coupon is measured using a
measuring microscope with a precision of better than 0.05 mil. Typically, eight to nine transverse
lines are measured perpendicular to the length of the coupon. The corrosion loss is calculated as
half of the difference of the measured widths between the original coupon and the reacted
coupon at the neck (glass-air interface) and at the half-down locations (the midpoint between the
glass surface and the lower end of the coupon). The errors associated with the measurements are
less than 0.1 mil.

 1.3.3 Melter Feed Rheology

 Samples of the melter feeds that were used for these tests were also subjected to
rheological characterization. The results from rheological characterization of a variety of other

 23

melter feeds and waste simulants, as well as the effects of a range of test variables, are described
in detail in a separate report [29]. Melter feeds were characterized using a Haake RS75
rheometer, which was equipped with either a Z40DIN or a FL22-SZ40 sensor per VSL SOPs
[24]. A typical set of measurements consists of identifying the flow characteristics of the slurry
by measuring the shear stress on the slurry at controlled shear rates and temperatures. In these
measurements, the shear rate values are preset and are increased stepwise from 0.01 s-1 to 200 s-1
(70 s-1 for FL22-SZ40) with a sufficient delay (typically 15 to 30 seconds) between steps to
ensure that the shear stress is allowed to fully relax and, therefore, is measured at equilibrium.
This approach is somewhat different from the "flow curve" approach in which the shear rate is
ramped up to some maximum value and then ramped back down to produce a hysteresis curve
that is dependent on the selected ramp rate. The viscosity of the sample as a function of the shear
rate is then calculated as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate. The yield stress data for the
melter feeds were measured using a controlled-stress mode in which the torque on the rotor was
slowly increased while the resulting deformation of the fluid was monitored. The discontinuity in
the measured deformation-torque curve was identified as the yield stress. It should be noted that
this direct measurement of the yield stress can be quite different from the value that is often
reported as the yield stress, which is obtained by extrapolation of the shear stress-shear rate curve
to zero shear rate. All of the measurements in this work were made at 25°C; previous work [29],
which examined a range of temperatures, showed a relatively weak effect of temperature.

1.4 DM100 Melter Systems

         1.4.1     DM100 Feed System

 A schematic diagram of the DM100 vitrification system is shown in Figure 1.1. The
melter feed is introduced in batches into a feed container that is mounted on a load cell for
weight monitoring. The feed is stirred with a variable speed mixer and constantly recirculated
except for periodic, momentary interruptions during which the weight is recorded. In typical
operation, the feed is introduced into the melter using a series of valves operated in a timed
sequence to mimic the operation of an ADS pump. However, the WTP Project directed the use of
a peristaltic pump for the present tests in order to provide a more uniform delivery of feed to the
melt surface. Feed was directed from the recirculation loop that extends to the top of the melter
then diverted to the peristaltic pump, which regulates the flow of feed through a Teflon-lined
feed line and water-cooled feed tube into the melter.

 1.4.2 DM100 Melter System

 Cross-sectional diagrams of the DM100-BL melter are shown in Figure 1.2.a-c. The
DM100-BL unit is a ceramic refractory-lined melter fitted with a total of five electrodes: two
pairs of opposing Inconel 690 plate electrodes and a bottom electrode. Power can be supplied in
either three-phase or single-phase configurations. All of the tests in the present work were
performed with the upper and lower electrodes on each side connected together and powered by
a single-phase supply; the bottom electrode was not powered. Melt pool agitation is achieved by
either a removable lance entering from the top of the melter or a permanent bubbler installed

 24

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

through the bottom electrode. In the present tests, the lance bubbler was used. The glass product
is removed from the melter by means of an airlift discharge system. The melter has a melt
surface area of 0.108 m2 and a variable glass inventory of between 110 kg, when only the bottom
pair of electrodes is used and about 180 kg when both pairs of electrodes are used.

 1.4.3 DM100 Off-Gas System

 For operational simplicity, the DM 100-BL melter is equipped with a dry off-gas
treatment system involving gas filtration operations only, as shown in Figure 1.1. Exhaust gases
leave the melter plenum through a film cooler device that minimizes the formation of solid
deposits. The film-cooler air has constant flow rate and its temperature is thermostatically
controlled. Consequently, the exhaust gases passing through the transition line (between the
melter and the first filtration device) can be sampled at constant temperature and airflow rate.
The geometry of the transition line conforms to the requirements of the 40-CFR-60 air sampling
techniques. Immediately downstream of the transition line are cyclonic filters equipped with
internal coarse filter elements followed by conventional pre-filters and High-Efficiency
Particulate Air (HEPA) filters. The temperatures of the cyclonic and the HEPA filters are held
above 100°C to prevent moisture condensation. The entire train of gas filtration operations is
duplicated and each train is used alternately. An induced draft fan completes the system.

 25

 SECTION 2.0
 WASTE SIMULANT AND GLASS FORMULATIONS

 The glass compositions used in these tests are derived from two sources: (i) those
previously formulated and tested at the crucible scale and which have properties that correspond
to the properties targeted in the Test Specification [22]; and (ii) variations on HLW98-86,
formulated from a simulated HLW C-106/AY-102 waste with products from Sr/TRU removal,
that was used to evaluate the effects of additive source and batching errors. Suitable glasses that
had been tested previously [3, 6] at the crucible scale were selected for extremes in viscosity,
electrical conductivity, tendency to form crystals, and for comparison to the variations on the
HLW98-86 glass composition. The HLW98-86 composition was processed previously on the
DM100 [10] and DM1200 [11, 17, 19] and, therefore, provides a good basis for comparison for
the current tests. Note that the target compositions of all glasses selected for testing do not contain
radionuclides, which is largely a consequence of the fact that the waste bases used in the design of
these glasses are either non-radioactive or very low in the concentrations of radioactive
components (e.g., HLW98-86 is based on a C-106/AY-102 waste with < 0.1 wt% uranium oxide).
Additionally, in the selection process, radioactive candidate glasses were not chosen for testing
since radionuclides would not be included in melter testing. Fifteen percent additive variations on
the HLW98-86 formulation were made and tested at the crucible scale prior to processing on the
DM100 [21]. Melter feeds were produced by NOAH Technologies Corporation, the supplier of
HLW simulant and feed samples used in previous testing on the DM-100 and DM-1200 melter
systems.

2.1 Formulation of Matrix and Algorithm Glasses

 HLW glasses that were previously developed and tested at VSL included the so-called
matrix glasses. The matrix glasses were designed specially to support the development of HLW
property-composition models, which are required to support HLW waste form qualification and
processing at the WTP. After development of constraints on the waste compositions, HLW glass
properties, and waste loading requirements, the test matrix was statistically designed to generate
glasses in order to optimally cover the experimental glass composition regions of interest. The
statistical design was performed jointly by VSL and Battelle Pacific Northwest Division
(PNWD). A staged approach was adopted in the testing of matrix glasses in order to allow the
incorporation of evolving information and data on waste compositions and process knowledge,
which resulted in a total of five matrices consisting of 207 HLW glasses (including replicates)
tested. The two matrix glasses selected for the current tests (HLW02-24 and -46) are from the
Initial Matrix, which was designed to cover the expected compositions of the waste from tanks
AZ-101, AZ-102, and C-106/AY-102. Reference [5] describes the constraints development,
statistical design process, and testing involved in the Initial Matrix testing. The final HLW
property-composition models will be used in the WTP HLW formulation algorithm to calculate
acceptable HLW glass compositions. At present, it is expected that the HLW formulation

 26

algorithm will be used to formulate HLW glass compositions for HLW processing at the WTP
because current WTP flow-sheet models and projections predict that the HLW feed compositions
will change continuously and the processing schedule will not allow sufficient time for glass
testing. The algorithm for calculating HLW glass compositions is being developed at the WTP. In
support of the HLW formulation algorithm development, VSL has performed tests on selected
glasses calculated by the preliminary algorithm. Descriptions of two of the algorithm glasses
selected to support the present testing, HLW-ALG-16 and HLW-ALG-17, are given in Reference
[4]. The glass HLW-ALG-16 was selected for its low concentration of Fe2O3 (4.4 wt%) and to
examine the effect of low electrical conductivity of the melt (0.23 S/cm at 1150°C). The glass
HLW-ALG-17 was selected because of its compositional extremes in Al2O3 (1.93 wt%) and NiO
(1.00 wt%); it was also chosen for its low melt viscosity (19 P at 1150°C). Table 2.1 summarizes
the compositions of the four HLW matrix and algorithm glasses selected for the current tests; it
also provides the targeted bounding glass properties or compositions for each of the selected
glasses.

 As noted above, the HLW matrix glasses were designed to support the development of
property-composition models. These glasses were statistically formulated to cover optimally the
glass composition regions of interest and were not individually based on any specific waste
composition. Consequently, in order to formulate the waste simulants and melter feeds for the
present tests, an assumption must be made about which (and how much) of the component oxides
in HLW02-26 and -46 originate from the wastes, with balance then presumed to be provided by
the glass forming additives. Based on waste and melter feed formulations employed in earlier
melter testing, the assumption used in the present work is that all glass oxides result from the
waste, with the exceptions of B2O3, Li2O, Na2O, SiO2, and ZnO. This is primarily due to the
generally negligible concentrations of boron, lithium, and zinc found in the wastes; their oxides
are therefore assumed to result solely from the respective glass formers. Sodium and silicon are
typically present in the wastes at somewhat higher concentrations. However, since SiO2 is used as
the silicon source in both the waste simulant and glass former mix, it is convenient to assume that
all SiO2 in the target glass derives from the additives. A few percent (≈ 3 wt%) of Na2O is
assumed to be present in the waste such that the sodium salts of various volatile components (e.g.,
nitrate) can be used as convenient sources of starting materials, with the remainder of Na2O
coming from the glass forming additives of borax and/or sodium carbonate. Additionally, in an
approach similar to that taken in earlier tests, noble metals (including silver) and minor
components (<0.02 wt% in glass) are omitted from the simulant formulation to minimize the
number of components, while concentrations of the volatile species (e.g., nitrate) are assumed to
be the same as those found in the C-106/AY-102 simulant (see Section 2.2). Finally, in order to
facilitate comparisons, the suspended solids content in the current simulant is assumed to be
identical to that used for most of the simulants tested previously (i.e., 20 wt%). Table 2.2 lists the
resulting compositions on an oxide basis of the HLW simulant, glass former additives, and the
target test glass to be employed for the testing of HLW02-24. Table 2.3 provides the chemical
compositions of the waste simulant and additives required to produce 1 MT of test glass. Tables
2.4 and 2.5 provide the corresponding information for testing of HLW02-46.

 In contrast to the situation described above, projections of waste compositions are the
starting points of algorithm glass calculations. Formulation of the waste simulants for the

 27

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

calculated algorithm glasses, therefore, makes use of the pre-defined waste compositions and
requires no extra assumptions. The IHLW algorithm results also include specification of the glass
former mix. Otherwise, the same approach was followed in formulating the simulants and melter
feeds for the algorithm glasses, including the omission of noble metals and minor components
(<0.02 wt% oxide in glass), identical volatile concentrations, and similar suspended solids
contents (20 wt%, which is equivalent to 21.5 wt% total solids). Tables 2.6 - 2.9 present the
relevant compositional information for the two algorithm glasses that were tested. Table 2.6 lists
the oxide compositions of the simulant, glass former mix, target glass, and the original algorithm
glass for HLW-ALG-16; Table 2.7 summarizes the chemical make-up of the waste simulant and
additives required to produce 1 MT of the target test glass. Tables 2.8 and 2.9 provide the
corresponding information for HLW-ALG-17.

2.2 HLW98-86 Glass Composition and Targeted Variations

        2.2.1    C-106/AY-102 Waste Simulant

 The composition of the C-106/AY-102 HLW simulant used in these tests was derived and
specified in a BNI Test Specification [30]. The C-106/AY-102 waste data and blending
assumptions stipulated in the Test Specification are different than those used in melter testing
with C-106/AY-102 simulants during Part B1 [31], the Semi-Integrated Pilot Plant (SIPP)
simulant produced at the Savannah River National Laboratory (SRNL) [10], and the “high waste
loading” composition [19]. Formulation of the C-106/AY-102 waste simulant used in this study
makes use of inventory data from the TFCOUP [32], calculated data from ACM modeling, and
analytical data on Cs- and Tc-removal eluates from LAW pretreatment [33]. In addition, products
of Sr/TRU removal from pretreatment of LAW are also included in the waste blend. As seen
below, blending of pretreatment products with the HLW solids increases the concentration of
selected components. This increase potentially can have important impact on glass formulations,
especially if the increased components can be incorporated in glass only at low concentrations.
However, with the current projected product compositions and blending ratios, waste blending
does not significantly impact formulation of an acceptable HLW glass. For example, the principal
effect of blending Tc-removal eluates is to increase sodium and chloride, but sodium is a
glass-forming additive while the chloride concentration is increased only to about 0.4 wt% (or
about 0.1 wt% in glass).

 Compositional summary of the waste streams and blended solids for the C-106/AY-102
HLW simulant is given in Table 2.10. The composition of the C-106/AY-102 Envelope D solids
(Stream FRP02) is based on the inventory data found in Revision 3A of the TFCOUP [32]. (Note
that the C-106/AY-102 simulant was first formulated for testing in 2002 and made use of the
latest information and data available at the time, hence the reference to a version of the TFCOUP
that has since been superseded. Newer revisions of TFCOUP have since become available but
they do not affect current testing since the present work is targeting specific glass compositions
and not waste compositions.) As seen in Table 2.10, in addition to updated information, Revision
3A of the TFCOUP also provides information on minor components that were not included in
earlier revisions [34] and the Best Basis Inventory (BBI) database (e.g., cadmium). The use of

 28

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

other data sources (e.g., HLW Feed Staging Plan [35]) to supplement the TFCOUP, as was done
in previous tests, is therefore no longer necessary. The ACM model calculates the composition of
the recycle stream (PWD01), which is then blended with the Envelope D solids based on the
expected daily processing rates (i.e., 3.79E+04 lb/day for Envelope D solids and 1.31E+03
lb/day for the recycle stream on a dry solid basis). The resulting material is concentrated and
pretreated through caustic leaching/water washing and ultra-filtration to produce the pretreated
HLW solids. The separation factors due to caustic leaching and ultra-filtration are given in Table
2.10. Note that some of the separation factors are larger than unity (many of which were ignored
in derivation of the waste composition, which is used as-provided [30] in the present work) and
that the ACM model predicts mass increases for Fe and Zr after ultra-filtration (75 lb/day and 68
lb/day, respectively) [30].

 To complete the simulant formulation, the pretreated HLW solids are blended with
wastes from LAW pretreatment. Similar to the blending scenario used in Part B1 tests [33],
Sr/TRU removal products from pretreatment of Envelope C wastes were added for these tests,
although the amounts of Sr and Mn (449 lb/day and 499 lb/day, respectively) blended are
considerably less than those used in earlier tests, which results in lower concentrations of SrO
and MnO in the current test glass (e.g., 0.92 wt% vs. 7.35 wt% for SrO) [31]. Analytical data on
eluates from Cs- and Tc-removal on an Envelope C sample (AN-102) [33] provide the
compositional bases for the respective ACM-model feed streams CNP12 and TEP12 although
that was not the case for the Sr/TRU stream. The blending proportions are determined by the
projected daily processing rate of sodium in the eluates (i.e., 2.02E+01 lb/day for Cs-removal
and 9.14E−01 lb/day for Tc-removal). It can be seen in Table 2.10 that waste blending primarily
leads to increase of manganese, strontium, sodium, chloride, and nitrate in the HLW simulant.

 The calculated composition of the blended HLW solids (HLP09b), which is shown in
Table 2.10, lists a total of 55 components. A few of the components, however, were left out of
the blended solids in the Test Specification [30] because of missing separation factors, low
concentrations and other unspecified reasons (e.g., Be, Co and Mo). In addition, similar to the
approach taken in previous melter tests, radionuclides, noble metals (including silver), and minor
components (< 0.02 wt% in glass on an oxide basis) are omitted from the simulant formulations.
The only significant radionuclide in Table 2.10 is uranium; it is omitted from the simulant and
glass formulations since it is present at very low concentration (< 0.1 wt% on an oxide basis).
Cesium and iodine are spiked for analytical purposes, at an amount equivalent to 0.05 wt% and
0.10 wt%, respectively, in glass. The resulting HLW simulant formulation, which is given in
Table 2.11, consists of 30 components, 26 of which are non-volatile (compared with 33 and 29,
respectively, for the previous C-106/AY-102 simulant [31]).

 2.2.2 Nominal HLW98-86 Glass and Melter Feed Formulations

 The glass composition developed for the C-106/AY-102 Envelope D solids and waste
blending scenario described in Section 2.2.1, HLW98-86, is presented in Table 2.11. On an oxide
basis, this glass has a total waste loading of 27.75 wt%, of which 25.13 wt% is Envelope D
waste. These can be compared with the respective values of 51.00 wt% and 39.42 wt% for

 29

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

HLW98-34, the reference glass used in Part B1 [31]. The difference is primarily due to the
presence of much more Na2O in the Part B1 simulant (20.61 wt% vs. 2.11 wt% for the current
simulant). The current target glass (HLW98-86) is also different from HLW98-34 in that it meets
the contract minimum component limit by incorporating 12.56 wt% of Fe2O3, instead of
> 21 wt% of (Al2O3+Fe2O3+ZrO2).

 Crucible melts of HLW98-86 and related formulations have been prepared and tested to
determine that the target glass meets the necessary processing requirements [3]. Heat treatment at
950 °C for over 70 hours of HLW98-86 results in a homogeneous dark brown glass that is free of
secondary phases. The viscosity and electrical conductivity measured at 1150°C for
HLW98-86AG, which has the same composition as HLW98-86 except with Ag2O excluded, are
44 P and 0.36 S/cm, respectively. Finally, the normalized PCT leach rates of HLW98-86 are (in
g/l) 0.806, 0.661, 0.645, and 0.395, respectively, for B, Li, Na, and Si; these values can be
compared with those for the reference glass, SRL-EA: 16.695, 9.565, 13.346 and 4.126. The
PCT leach rates for HLW98-86 in g/(m2-day) are 0.058, 0.047, 0.046, and 0.028. The target glass
formulation for these tests, which is also given in Table 2.11, differs slightly from HLW98-86,
with the removal of silver and the addition of small amounts of cesium and iodine.

 The same waste composition used to formulate HLW98-86 was supplied to the
preliminary algorithm for calculating HLW glass compositions at the WTP [1]. The composition
calculated by the algorithm is compared to the HLW98-86 composition in Table 2.12. With the
exception of aluminum and zinc, all of the values calculated by the algorithm are within six
percent of their respective values in HLW98-86. The original HLW98-86 composition was used
for the present tests because of the large amount of existing test data that are available for
comparison.

 The additional constituents required to form the target test glass from the C-106/AY-102
HLW simulant are aluminum, boron, lithium, sodium, silicon, and zinc. The additives that are
the sources for the glass-forming chemicals are selected based on previous testing and WTP
Project approved sources. Table 2.13 lists the starting materials and amounts required to produce
the target C-106/AY-102 simulant and melter feed. Note that all of the organic carbon is
assumed to be oxalate and that only 1.23 g/100 g oxide of carbonate is present in the simulant
instead of the required 1.29 g/100 g oxide. The small discrepancy in carbonate does not impact
the tests since much greater amounts are present in the glass forming additives. The suspended
solids in the simulant is assumed to be 20 wt%, which is equivalent to 21.49 wt% total solids,
based on the data from AZ-102 testing [36]. The theoretical glass yield of the resulting feed is
372 g of glass/kg of feed (about 485-550 g/l of feed, dependent on feed density).

 2.2.3 Batching Variations

 Glass and feed compositions modeling the +/-15% batching errors in GFC additions are
based on the HLW98-86 glass and feed composition described in Section 2.2.2. The results of
crucible testing of these glasses have been reported earlier [21]. The glass composition in the
feed as well as the projected glass pool composition and calculated glass properties at the end of

 30

each test are given in Table 2.14. The glass pool composition was calculated assuming a constant
glass production rate of 1050 kg/m2/day. The glass properties calculated from the glass pool and
feed compositions are very similar at the end of the tests and would become increasingly closer if
the actual production rate is greater than the conservative rate used in the calculation. The
nominal test that is conducted in between the fifteen percent variation tests consists of two test
segments: the first with the nominal feed composition shown in Table 2.11 and the second with
replacement of borax with sodium carbonate and boric acid.

2.3 Physical Properties of Feed Samples

 2.3.1 General Properties

 Feed samples from each test were analyzed to confirm physical properties. Samples were
taken during melter testing from an inline sampling port. Sample names, sampling dates, and
measured properties are given in Table 2.15. Measured glass yields deviate from target values by
only 0.3 to 3.5 percent, validating the use of target values for calculating production rates during
these tests. Measured density values increase with increasing glass yield, as expected. Except for
Test B2 samples, measured pH values spanned a narrow range between 10.15 and 10.68 as a
result of the same additives and volatile waste component assumptions being used for all the
feeds. The replacement of borax by boric acid and sodium carbonate as additives for boron and
sodium in Test B2 feed resulted in a drop in pH of approximately half a unit.

 2.3.2 Rheology

 Samples from the later tests in the first test matrix were analyzed to determine the effects
of additive type and batching errors on rheological properties. Rheograms for the melter feeds,
which show the feed viscosity versus shear rate, are presented in Figure 2.1; measured values of
viscosity at selected shear rates and the yield stress values are shown in Table 2.16. Notice that
the baseline melter feed, based on samples from both the current and previous tests, is much less
viscous than melter feed from the three tests with the HLW98-86 variations. The yield stress
values exhibited the same trends as those described for the viscosity at low shear rates. The
increase in viscosity with either the addition or subtraction of 15 percent of additives was
certainly unexpected. Accordingly, measurements were made on two additional samples: (i)
another sample from Test C (15% deficient in additives) and (ii) a sample of a new batch of the
baseline feed that was recently blended by NOAH (for the tests in the second matrix). Thus we
can compare three sets of values for baseline melter feed samples: the average value from
DM100 and DM1200 tests conducted about fours ago [11], BLR-F-149A used in Test B1 and
derived from residual feed from about four years ago, and BLU-F-149A recently received from
NOAH for Matrix 2 tests. The measured rheological properties of these feeds are very similar,
confirming the analysis of the sample from Test B1. These results also demonstrate that aging of
this feed for several years had no appreciable effect on feed properties and that feed made to the
same recipe from different chemical batches yields very similar rheological properties. The
analysis of the second feed sample from Test C, BLS-F-98A, confirms the results on the first
sample measured from this test. The additional data, therefore, tend to confirm rather than

 31

resolve the surprising rheological response to GFC variations around this baseline melter feed.
The results suggest that the rheological properties of the baseline feed with either a fifteen
percent surplus or deficit are indistinguishable from each other but substantially different than
those for the baseline feed with higher viscosities and yield stresses. Furthermore, substitution of
boric acid and sodium carbonate also resulted in an increase in viscosity and yield stress, perhaps
due to the lowering of the pH. Taken together, the rheology measurements indicate that the
baseline feed recipe is uniquely formulated to produce the least viscous feed such that all of the
tested deviations yielded a more viscous feed.

 32

 SECTION 3.0
 MELTER OPERATIONS

 Melter tests were conducted on the DM100-BL between 3/5/07 and 6/22/07. These tests
produced over 3200 kg of glass from almost nine metric tons of feed. The nominally 100-hour
tests were divided as follows:

 • Test W: High viscosity target (112 P at 1150oC) obtained by processing HLW02-24
 matrix glass composition. Also targeted were high glass concentrations of MnO and
 SrO at 5 wt% each. Sugar was added near test midpoint at 10 g per liter of feed to
 reduce foaming that was significantly reducing glass production rate.

 • Test X: Low viscosity target (19 P at 1150oC) obtained by processing HLW-ALG-17
 algorithm glass composition. Also targeted were high MnO at 6.91 wt% and low Al2O3
 at 1.93 wt%, as well as maximum NiO at 1.0 wt%.

 • Test Y: High electrical conductivity target (0.68 S/cm at 1200oC) obtained by
 processing HLW-ALG-16 algorithm glass composition. Also targeted was low Fe2O3 at
 4.4 wt%.

 • Test Z: Low electrical conductivity target (0.23 S/cm at 1150oC) obtained by
 processing HLW02-46 matrix glass composition. Also targeted was a T1% temperature
 of 963oC. Sugar was added near test midpoint at 10 g per liter of feed to reduce
 foaming that was significantly reducing glass production rate. This test was followed by
 nearly 1000 hours of idling near the T1% temperature to determine crystallization
 characteristics.

 • Test A: HLW98-86 glass composition with a 15% surplus of additives.

 • Test B: Conducted in two segments with HLW98-86 glass composition, the first with
 nominal additives and the second with replacement of borax with boric acid and sodium
 carbonate. Additional run time was added to the first segment in order to compensate
 for time lost to repair a defective bubbler during the test.

 • Test C: HLW98-86 glass composition with a 15% deficit of additives.

 Summaries of the tests are provided in Table 3.1. Steady state production rates for all the
tests are illustrated in Figure 3.1. Attempts were made to replicate the melter configuration and
operating conditions used for previous tests with HLW simulants [8-12, 15, 19]. These
conditions include a near complete cold cap, which is between 80-95% melt surface coverage for
the DM100 since a 100% cold cap tends to lead to "bridging" in smaller melters. The bubbling
rate was fixed at 9 lpm and the feed rate was adjusted to maintain a complete cold cap; this is in

 33

contrast to most previous tests where the production rate was fixed between 1000 to 1050
kg/m2/day and the bubbling rate was adjusted to maintain the complete cold cap [8, 9, 11, 12,
15]. The approach used in the present tests permits the direct comparison of production rates
between each feed currently tested as well as with some previously tested [10, 19]. At the
beginning of each test, water was fed onto the melt surface to cool down the melt surface prior to
the introduction of melter feed. The melt pool was lowered to the minimum glass volume
(approximately 110 kg of glass) prior to tests with different compositions in order to accelerate
the compositional turnover.

3.1 Feed Processing and Glass Production Rates

 Each of the various melter feeds were mixed, pumped, and processed with no difficulties.
Interruptions in feeding of up to 50 minutes per test transpired during feed transfers, adjustments
to the feed line in the peristaltic pump as a result of wear from the pump rollers, and the time
required for connecting the upper pair of electrodes. Longer interruptions occurred during Test X
to clear blockages in the film cooler region resulting from the high production rates obtained
with that feed. Foaming on the glass surface led to long interruptions during Tests W and Z as a
result of various unsuccessful attempts to achieve sustainable cold cap conditions with
production rates of at least 900 kg/m2/day. Based on the successful use of sugar to control
foaming observed with highly oxidizing high-nitrate feeds (e.g., at West Valley, the M-Area
mixed waste vitrification facility at Savannah River, and the WTP LAW Pilot Melter), sugar
additions were evaluated for the present feeds, despite their low nitrate content. After sugar at
10 g per liter was added to the feed at the Test W and Test Z midpoints, cold cap conditions
became more favorable, foaming subsided, and production rates increased. This concentration of
sugar was selected based on results from previous tests that processed feed with the same
concentration of nitrates, nitrites, organic carbon, and sugar and resulted in divalent iron
concentrations within the desired range (less than ~10% of total iron) [17]. Sugar additions were
not employed for any of the other tests. Cold cap conditions during the other tests were very
similar to each other and similar to those in previous tests with HLW feeds [8-12, 19], with some
adherence of feed to the melter walls resulting in some formation of “shelves” and “bridges”.
Spikes in feed rate often occurred immediately after feed transfers due to adjustments in tank
mixer speeds and pump settings. No foamy glass was observed in the glass discharge and no
foam was observed on the melt pool surface or cold cap except in Tests W and Z prior to the
addition of sugar to the feed.

 Production rates, depicted over the course of each of the tests in Figures 3.2.a – 3.2.g,
varied in response to the compositional changes that led to the high and low glass viscosity and
electrical conductivity cases but changed little in response to simulant to additive ratio and
additive type. The steady-state production rates ranged between 900 to 1600 kg/m2/day (with
sugar additions to Tests W and Z) and bracketed the range of steady-state production rates (1100
to 1400 kg/m2/day) measured on other WTP HLW waste streams processed on the DM100 under
similar conditions [8, 10, 19]. Comparison of results from Tests W through Z shows that the
melts with lower viscosity (and higher electrical conductivity) processed faster. The less viscous
and more conductive glasses generally have higher concentrations of alkali and lower
concentrations of aluminum; however, the glass composition with the highest aluminum

 34

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

concentration, HLW-ALG-16, processed at a rate a third higher than the more viscous and less
conductive formulations. Sugar additions during Tests W and Z allowed a near doubling of
production rates from about 500 to 900 kg/m2/day. The foaming during the first half of these
tests was partially mitigated by the addition of 10 grams of sucrose per liter of feed when cold
cap conditions improved appreciably. As noted above, the glass processing difficulties from
foaming occurred with the more viscous melts, presumably due to the inability of gas to readily
escape from the melt. Foaming did not increase with glass manganese content and, in fact, the
glass with the highest manganese concentration, HLW-ALG-17, processed at the highest rate of
any of the compositions tested, with no foaming problems. Tests processing the HLW98-86
formulation with fifteen percent GFC batching errors and with boric acid and soda ash replacing
borax all resulted in steady-state production rates between 1000 and 1050 kg/m2/day, indicating
that these changes do not have a substantial effect on processing rates. Since these feed
variations resulted in significant changes in the rheological properties of the feeds, the test results
also indicate that the differences in feed rheological properties did not affect production rate.
These measured production rates are consistent with the rate of about 1150 kg/m2/day measured
previously for this feed on the DM100 while bubbling at the higher rate of 17.2 lpm [11] as
opposed to the 9 lpm used throughout the present tests. The decrease in production rate during
Test B1 between 13 and 46 hours run time is attributable to a loose connection between the
bubbler and air supply that was not apparent in the electronic readout. After the problem was
identified and corrected, additional run time was added to the test in order to verify the steady-
state rate of 1000 kg/m2/day that was obtained at the beginning of the test.

 The occurrence of foaming for some feeds but not in others, the significant impact of
foaming on melt rate, and the drastic improvement afforded by sugar additions are all
noteworthy. As pointed out above, the use of reductants in general and sugar in particular is a
well established method of foaming control in waste vitrification. This is especially important for
high nitrate feeds, which are highly oxidizing. In particular, a procedure for specifying the
amount of sugar needed for any particular WTP LAW batch composition has been developed
and validated through extensive VSL testing for the WTP. In contrast, because of the historical
presumption of low nitrate concentrations, no comparable scheme has been developed for WTP
HLW vitrification. Despite this, addition of sugar was a natural response to the foaming observed
during testing based on extensive prior experience with many high nitrate (i.e., relatively
oxidizing) feeds. Sugar additions tend to facilitate reduction reactions to occur in the cold cap
instead of the glass melt, thereby allowing gas to escape the cold cap region without causing the
glass pool to foam. If the molten glass is more viscous, gas escape is further hampered,
increasing the tendency towards foaming when the reduction reactions occur in the molten glass.
Thus, melt viscosity is also an important parameter. Sugar additions also change the redox state
of the glass melt. This reduces the extent of transition metal redox reactions that may occur with
temperature changes; typically, temperature increases in highly oxidized melts cause transition
metal (e.g. Mn, Fe) reduction with release of tiny oxygen bubbles that may cause foaming. In
view of the significant effects on melt rate, further work is recommended on understanding and
controlling foaming in low-nitrate WTP HLW feeds and on developing a practical algorithm for
specifying sugar additions based on feed composition.

 35

3.2 Measured Parameters

 Melter operating conditions were held constant throughout the tests in order to facilitate
comparison of the results with those from previous tests [8-12, 15, 19]. Electronically recorded
operational measurements are provided in Table 3.2. The target bubbling rate of 9 lpm was
maintained throughout the tests except during a portion of Test B1. Glass temperatures are
shown in Figures 3.3.a – 3.3.g, plenum temperatures in Figures 3.4.a – 3.4.g, electrode
temperatures in Figures 3.5.a – 3.5.g, and glass pool resistance and electrode power in Figures
3.6.a – 3.6.g. Measured test average glass temperatures in the bulk glass (5” and 10” from the
bottom) were within 9°C of the target glass temperature of 1150°C during the tests. Glass
temperatures at higher locations in the glass pool were lower than the target due to the intentional
varying of the glass pool level at the beginning and end of each test as well as the expected
temperature gradients near the cold cap. Also, adjusting power to achieve this temperature was a
balance between the bottom and top halves of the melt pool with the upper portion of the melt
pool being 20-30°C cooler. Plenum temperatures typically ranged from 350 to 500°C, indicating
that the target of a complete cold cap was obtained throughout the tests. Higher plenum
temperatures occurred during Test X at the highest production rate due to the rapid incorporation
of the cold cap into the melt. Plenum temperatures monitored by the exposed thermocouple were
about 25°C higher than the plenum temperature monitored in the thermowell due in part to
shelves forming across the walls of the melter shielding the thermowell. Similar to the glass pool
during steady state processing, the lower pair of electrodes was a little hotter than the upper pair
of electrodes. The side electrode temperatures increased about 350ºC from their initial
near-plenum temperatures to about 1050ºC over the first day of feeding as the melt level was
raised. The temperature difference across the melter between the East and West electrodes was
near zero to about 35ºC, with no clear pattern with respect to feeding and operating conditions.
The bottom electrode, which was not powered, typically ranged in temperature from 700 to
800ºC and was about 300ºC colder than the side electrodes.

 Power supplied to the electrodes and glass pool resistance varied in response to glass and
feed compositions as well as production rates. Power supplied to the electrodes ranged from 17
to 25 kW during steady processing and was lower during non-steady-state periods with lower
production rates, such as in Tests W and Z prior to the addition of sugar, as well as during a
portion of Test B1 as a result of bubbler malfunction. Utilization of power was higher in Tests X
and Y than other tests due to the higher production rates; power normalized to glass production
rate shows the opposite trend. Glass resistance ranged between 0.05 and 0.15 ohms during
testing and was higher at the beginning of each test prior to energizing the top electrode pair.
Changes in resistance in response to changes in the glass composition are best observed in Test
X as the resistance drops from 0.14 to 0.06 ohms during the transition to the low viscosity
composition. Test average glass resistance in Tests W and Z targeting high viscosity and low
conductivity glasses were about 30 percent higher than in Tests X and Y targeting low viscosity
and high conductivity glasses. The addition of sugar to the feed in Test W resulted in a drop in
glass resistance of about 30%, whereas the same sugar addition to the feed in Test Z appeared to
have little effect on glass resistance. Typically, the melt pool electrical resistance increases in
response to the presence of glass bubbles in the melt. Thus, removal of such bubbles
(suppression of foaming) can lead to a decrease of the resistance. It is also possible that reduction

 36

of specific components such as Fe can change the resistivity and hence the glass pool resistance.
Review of the two glass compositions, however, shows no obvious reason for the difference in
their behavior. Differences in glass pool resistance were small in response to additive type and
waste to additive ratio.

 The gas temperature at the film cooler averaged between 268-288ºC and depends on the
plenum temperature, the amount of added film cooler air, and the temperature of the added film
cooler air. A small drop of ten degrees or less in gas temperature was observed across the
(insulated) transition line; the high temperature is maintained in order to prevent condensation in
the downstream filtration units. The discharge chamber temperature was typically maintained
above 950ºC to prevent crystallization in the chamber and facilitate the discharging of glass.

3.3 Idling Test to Determine Crystal Settling Rates

 A prolonged idling period at a glass temperature of about 950ºC was conducted to
determine the extent of glass crystallization at these extreme conditions. A sampling boat was
fabricated from Inconel, attached to a lance entering through the top of the melter, and lowered
to the melter bottom. A picture of the sampling boat is given in Figure 3.7. The sampling boat is
a cylinder of 7/8” internal diameter and 31/4” high. The idling occurred immediately after
conducting Test Z; that test used the HLW02-46 composition, which has a T1% temperature of
963ºC. The sampling boat lance was installed in the bubbler lance location and, therefore, no
bubbling occurred during the idling period. The depth of the glass pool at the start of this idling
period was about 30 inches. A summary of the nearly one thousand hour idling period and
measurements of glass pool conditions is provided in Table 3.3. Glass temperatures, plenum
temperatures, electrode temperature, and electrode power are depicted for the idling period in
Figures 3.8 – 3.10. The glass temperature profile during idling is similar to that during feed
processing: hottest in the center of the glass pool and coldest near the surface and bottom of the
melt pool. The target idling glass temperature of 950ºC was approximated in these colder regions
and in the airlift. The sampling boat was positioned between the thermocouple five inches from
the melter floor and the bottom electrode and therefore the temperature in the sampling boat was
between these two temperatures (950 and 800°C). Since the thermocouple is contained within the
non-energized bottom electrode, not on the surface of the electrode in contact with the glass, the
temperature of the glass in the boat is probably closer to the measured glass temperature five
inches from the bottom than that within the bottom electrode. Glass temperatures increased
gradually during the idling period requiring a small decrease in power at about 625 hours idling.
The abrupt rise in plenum temperature corresponds to a dip sampling event that disrupted a crust
(a cool glass layer) that had formed on the melt pool surface. Side electrode temperatures were
about 100ºC below those in the center of the glass pool; the bottom electrode temperature was
about 800ºC throughout the idling period once the glass temperatures had stabilized. Power
supplied to the electrodes was constant at between 8.9 and 8.6 kW after thermal stabilization.

 Samples were obtained throughout and at the end of the idling period for determination of
crystal content. During the idling period, simultaneous dip and glass discharge samples were
taken for analysis (see Section 4.7). The sampling boat was removed at the conclusion of the

 37

idling period to retrieve glass for analysis. A picture of the sampling boat after removing external
glass from the sampling boat is provided in Figure 3.11. The sampling boat was cut from the
lance and cut away from the glass down the length of the cylinder to provide a sample for
analysis. Pictures of the top and bottom of the glass removed for analysis are provided in Figures
3.12 and 3.13.

 38

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-07R1220-1, Rev. 0

 SECTION 4.0
 GLASS PRODUCTS

 Nearly three and a quarter metric tons of glass was discharged in these tests. The glass
was discharged from the melter periodically into 5-gallon carbon steel pails using an airlift
system. The discharged product glass was sampled at the end of each test by removing sufficient
