glass from the top of the cans for total inorganic analysis. Product glass masses, discharge date,
and analysis performed are given in Table 4.1. Glass samples were also obtained by dipping a
rod into the glass pool at the beginning and end of each test. These "dip samples" underwent
visual examination to detect the presence of separate secondary phases.

4.1 Feed and Glass Compositional Verification

 Discharge glass samples were crushed and analyzed directly by XRF. The target values
for boron and lithium oxides, which are not determined by XRF, were used for normalizing the
XRF data to 100 wt%. Comparison of the XRF analyzed compositions of discharged glass
samples form the end of each test are compared to target compositions and feed sample analysis
in Tables 4.2 – 4.8. The majority of the XRF analysis results compared very favorably to their
corresponding target values; however, the magnitude and number of deviations was greater than
observed in past studies with simulated HLW feeds prepared by the same vendor [37]. The
element with the greatest variation from target concentrations was aluminum, with surpluses that
increased with decreasing target concentrations. Alumina surpluses have been observed
previously in HLW melter feeds [37] although not of the magnitude occurring in the current
samples. A review of vendor batching sheets showed no discrepancies. A small fraction of the
high bias may be attributable to contamination during grinding of the glass samples in a
porcelain mortar and pestle since slightly lower values were obtained for samples ground in
tungsten carbide mortar. However, the majority of the deviation would appear to be due to
alumina contamination in the source chemicals, which would be consistent with its relative
increase with decreasing alumina content. The extent and source of contamination can only be
determined by analysis of the source chemicals. Based on the long history of similar purchase
from this vendor, routine performance of such analyses is not considered cost effective; however,
the WTP may need to include more extensive mineral source batch analyses than currently
planned in the procurement requirements. Deficiencies in manganese of between eight and
nineteen relative percent were observed in two of the compositions. Similar deficiencies have
been observed in some of the melter feeds processed previously [11, 14] and, therefore, is
probably attributable to variable purity of reagents obtained by the vendor. The feed and product
glass sample analyses were in good agreement for most constituents; however, the lack of
complete melt pool turnover for Tests W, Z, and A resulted in some disparities between the two
types of samples. Potassium, titanium, and sulfur, which are absent or at very low concentrations
in the target compositions, were measured in the glass and feed samples at above target
concentrations as a result of trace contamination in the NOAH feeds. The volatile elements

 39

selenium, arsenic, chlorine, iodine, and thallium were measured at lower than target
concentrations as a result of loss during crucible melting and melter processing.

 Corroborative analysis using DCP on solutions of acid dissolved glass was performed on
glasses from the end of each test; the results are compared to the XRF analysis in Table 4.9. The
first glass discharged during testing, BLP-G-144A, was also analyzed to determine the boron and
lithium content of the glass. The closeness of the DCP boron and lithium analysis to the target,
with deviations of less than six and ten percent, respectively, validates the use of the target boron
and lithium concentrations for normalizing the XRF data. Values measured using the wet
chemical method compare favorably with the XRF analysis and target composition for most
oxides. Many of the alumina concentrations are lower and therefore closer to the target
concentrations. Soda concentrations determined by the DCP method are consistently less than
those measured by the XRF, as has been observed previously [10, 37, 38], and the XRF values
are to be preferred.

4.2 Compositional Trends

 All discharged glass samples were subjected to XRF analysis; Table 4.10 provides XRF
analytical results for all glasses. Compositional trends for selected oxides shown in Figures 4.1.a
- 4.5.b illustrate the many differences in composition of the glass formulations tested. Generally,
many abrupt changes in concentrations are observed between matrix and algorithm glasses due
to major differences in the formulations as opposed to gradual changes while processing the
HLW98-86 variations. The breaks shown in the data in between the first four tests occur as a
result of lowering the melt pool at the end of each test followed by feeding the melter with a
glass equivalent of about 100 kg of the subsequent composition prior to the first discharge. The
melt pool composition at the start of testing was much lower in toxic metals, iron, manganese,
strontium, zirconium, and zinc than the HLW02-24 glass formulation and, therefore, the
concentrations of these elements increased throughout the first test. Other compositional trends
include the increases in transition metals and alkalis at the expense of aluminum and zirconium
during the transition to the HLW-ALG-17 low viscosity formulation. Most of these trends were
reversed in the subsequent test targeting the HLW-ALG-16 high conductivity formulation.
Transition metal and silicon concentrations increase at the expense of sodium and aluminum to
achieve the HLW02-46 glass composition preceding the extended idling period. During tests
with the HLW98-86 variations, there are gradual increases in oxides from the waste such as iron,
manganese, toxic metals, and magnesium at the expense of oxides from additives such as silicon
and zinc as the glass pool transitions from the composition with a fifteen percent surplus of
additives to a fifteen percent deficit of additives.

4.3 Iron Oxidation State

 The iron redox state of glass samples from the end of tests in which sugar was added was
determined using an AMI MS 1200 Mössbauer spectrometer by examining the locations and
intensities of the iron Mössbauer absorptions. This analytical procedure was not planned for

 40

samples generated during these tests. However, addition of reductants changes the oxidation state
of transition metals which in turn have the potential to change glass properties. In addition, the
measured response of the glass redox state to a given sugar addition provides valuable
information for defining suitable reductant addition protocols for WTP HLW vitrification. The
intent of the sugar additions to the feed was to reduce foaming in order to increase the glass
production rate while not over-reducing the glass pool (estimated to correspond to ~10% or less
of total iron as divalent iron). The same sugar additions to Tests W and Z resulted in similar
improvements in production rates but somewhat different impacts on glass redox: divalent iron
as a percent of total iron measured by Mossbauer spectroscopy in glass samples from Tests W
and Z were 13.6 and 5.4 percent, respectively. The difference may be due in part to the lower
concentration of total iron (8 vs. 10 wt% oxide) in the glass composition used in Test W;
however, that test also had the higher level of manganese (5 vs. 3.5 wt% as MnO). The waste
loading is also higher in the feed used in Test Z. Previously, melter tests conducted with various
HLW simulants and 10 grams per liter sugar resulted in a wide range of divalent iron
concentrations: AZ-102 – 7.6 percent [9], C-106/AY-102 – 3 to 7 percent [17], and AZ-101 - 11
to 29 percent [17]. In these tests, sugar was added per WTP direction to feeds that had previously
been processed without exhibiting any foaming. Unlike LAW feeds which contain high
concentrations of oxidants such as nitrates, no algorithm currently exists for determining the rate
of sugar addition to control foaming or to achieve a particular range of iron oxidation states in
the product glass. Although not directly measured, the manganese is assumed to be almost
entirely divalent based on the measured iron oxidation state and the manganese and iron
oxidation state measurements made on other similar glasses [39, 40]. The reduction of these
glasses did not have a measurable impact on glass durability as measured by TCLP (see Section
4.4) or other measured glass properties.

4.4 Glass Durability

 Glass discharge samples from the end of each test were evaluated for TCLP response
(See Section 1.3.2.5). Samples from these tests were selected due to the relatively high
concentrations of toxic metals in the target compositions as compared to other glasses given in
the Test Plan [20]. In Table 4.11, the TCLP results are compared to the WTP delisting limits [41,
42, 43] and to the Universal Treatment Standard (UTS) limits, though many of the latter do not
apply. All TCLP leachate concentrations for the melter glasses are less than 1 mg/l and less than
the respective WTP delisting and UTS limits. The measured concentrations in leachates from the
melter glasses are comparable to, and often less than, the measured concentrations in leachates
from comparable crucible glasses.

4.5 Comparison of Target and Measured Properties for Algorithm and Matrix Glasses

 Glasses from the end of feeding periods from Tests W, X, Y and Z were also analyzed for
viscosity and electrical conductivity to compare to target values obtained previously from the
analysis of crucible glasses. Data given in Table 4.12 show that the measured glass properties
from the discharged glasses approximate the target values in most cases. For Test X, the

 41

viscosity was 10 poise above the target, due perhaps to the aluminum surplus and the lack of
complete turnover due to the slow production rate. The test data from these four tests suggest
that the production rate would have been even higher in Test X at the target viscosity.

4.6 Glass Pool Samples

 Glass dip samples were obtained primarily to ascertain whether secondary phases had
formed on the surface of the glass melt. Table 4.13 provides a listing of all dip samples taken
with sampling dates, sample names, and observations of secondary phases observed. No
secondary phases were observed prior to or subsequent to any of the tests.

4.7 Analysis of Glass Samples from Idling Period

 Glass samples from the idling period described in Section 3.3 are listed in Table 4.14
with sample names, sample types, and sampling dates. Crystalline phase determinations on dip
and discharge glasses were made using the same procedures used on crucible glasses for the
determination of one-percent crystal fraction temperature (T1%). The boat sample was sectioned
and analyzed at ten locations from 1 to 10 mm from the bottom of the sampling boat.
Micrographs from each successive height from the bottom are given in Figures 4.6 – 4.15. The
extent of measured crystallinity in the dip and discharge samples does not increase with idling
time, indicating either that crystallization is not occurring over time or that the crystals that form
are settling out of the bulk glass and air lift. Also, the results from these two types of sample are
indistinguishable, suggesting the glass pool is relatively homogeneous with respect to crystal
phases. Conversely, analysis of the sample taken from the boat shows an increase in crystal
volume percentage of nearly two orders of magnitude from the bulk glass to 1 mm from the
melter floor, as illustrated in Figure 4.16 which shows two different regressed trend lines through
the data. Most of this increase occurs within 1 cm from the melter floor as the crystal content
increases from 0.6 to about 43 volume percent. It is possible that this situation arises from the
settling out of crystals that form in the bulk melt pool, leading to their accumulation in this
bottom layer. The stratification could be the result of hindered settling and the increase in melt
viscosity towards the bottom due to the decreasing temperature, in addition to the likely increase
in the effective viscosity with increasing crystal content. Alternatively, the data may indicate that
crystallization in the bulk glass is insignificant and that crystallization is significant only at the
very bottom of the melter in the coldest regions. While it is difficult to determine which of these
effects is dominant, increased crystallization in the colder regions almost certainly plays a
significant role. The data in Figure 4.16 suggest an approximate inverse dependence of the
volume percent crystals on the height. The crystalline phases are iron and chromium rich spinels
with lesser amounts of zinc, manganese, and aluminum, which is consistent with crystalline
phases observed in similar HLW glass formulations. However, it should be noted that Inconel
corrosion products, particular of chromium, may also play a role.

 In view of the boat geometry, any mixing between the glass in the boat and the melt pool
is likely to be limited. In the extreme case of no mixing, it is useful to estimate whether the

 42

observed amount of crystals in the boat could have formed solely from the glass volume in the
boat or whether instead one has to invoke a significant contribution from sedimentation from the
glass pool above. Integration of the data in Figure 4.16 (assuming 0 vol% crystals at heights
above 10 mm, consistent with the measured value at 10 mm) yields ~1.8 vol% crystals averaged
across the volume of glass in the boat. Since the T1% value for this glass is about 963oC, this
amount of crystallization is not inconsistent with formation in situ. Elemental mass balances
between the residual glass and the crystalline phases could be used to further investigate this
point.

 43

 SECTION 5.0
 MONITORED OFF-GAS EMISSIONS

5.1 Particulate Sampling

 The melter exhaust was sampled for metals/particles according to 40-CFR-60 Methods 3,
5, and 29 at steady-state operating conditions during each test segment. The concentrations of
off-gas species that are present as particulates and gaseous species that are collected in impinger
solutions were derived from laboratory data on solutions extracted from air samples (filters and
various solutions) together with measurements of the volume of air sampled. Particulate
collection required isokinetic sampling, which entails removing gas from the exhaust at the same
velocity that the air is flowing in the duct (40-CFR-60, Methods 1-5). Typically, a sample size of
30 dscf was taken at a rate of between 0.5 and 0.75 dscfm. Total particulate loading was
determined by combining gravimetric analysis of the standard particle filter and chemical
analysis of probe rinse solutions. An additional impinger containing 2 N NaOH was added to the
sampling train to ensure complete scrubbing of all acid gases and, particularly, iodine. The
collected materials were analyzed using DCP-AES for the majority of the constituents and ion
chromatography (IC) for anions. Melter emission fluxes are compared to feed fluxes in Table
5.1. Notice the distinction that is made between constituents sampled as particles and as "gas".
The "gaseous" constituents are operationally defined as those species that are scrubbed in the
impinger solutions after the air stream has passed through a 0.3 µm heated filter. All eight
samples are well within the 90 – 110% limits for isokinetic sampling.

 Particulate emissions from the melter ranged over an order of magnitude, from 0.3 to
3.37 percent of feed solids; however, results from five of the eight samples were within the
narrow range of 0.63 to 0.89 percent of feed solids. This narrow range of solids carryover is well
within the range measured on the same melter for HLW AZ-102 simulants (0.57 - 1.47 percent)
[19] and very similar to results from tests that processed C-106/AY-102 simulants (0.61 to 0.81
percent) [10]. The carryover values outside of previously measured ranges (2.59 percent for Test
W, 0.3 percent for Test B2, and 3.37 percent for Test C) are probably attributable to transient
changes in the cold cap since the operational parameters were kept constant during each test and
the compositional variations are not extreme. The latter is particularly true when comparing
results from Test C with those from Tests A, B1, and B2. The process data (e.g., positive
pressure spikes) do not show any obvious indication of transient cold cap changes during Test C
sampling. A notation was made in the notebook that initially during sampling, feed was bubbling
on a shelf then the shelf slowly began to dissolve. This level of cold cap change however is not
anticipated to have caused the high measured particulate emission rate. Measured particulate
carryover is the average over a nominally one-hour sampling period, unless the filter becomes
occluded with particulate forcing a shorter sampling time, as was the case while sampling during
Test C. Although normally not required to illustrate emission trends on the DM100 [9], triplicate
sampling is often performed to identify outliers. Carryover of non-volatile elements such as
silicon and aluminum, which are derived in part from the chemical analysis of rinse solutions and

 44

dissolved particulate on filters, is also elevated for Tests W and C, supporting the gravimetric
analysis of total particulate carryover. As expected, the feed elements emitted at the lowest
melter DF were clearly halides, sulfur, and selenium. Some of the reported sulfur DF values are
lower than actual due to the discernable but unquantifiable trace sulfur contamination in the feed
(see Section 4.1). Also, thallium, which was only contained in the feed recipe used in Test Z, was
almost totally volatilized from the feed and glass. Other elements exhibiting volatile behavior
include arsenic, chromium, and boron. Carryover for elements at low feed concentrations, and
particularly common elements such as calcium and magnesium, often exhibit a higher calculated
than actual volatility due to trace contamination of the feed. Emissions of chlorine and fluorine
were measured during all tests, even though they were not included in all feed recipes. These
halogens have frequently been observed in melter emissions when not present in feed recipe or at
levels exceeding the amount in the feed recipe due to their ubiquity in raw materials, presence in
tap water, and their high volatility at glass melting temperatures. Sufficient iodine was detected
in the basic impinger in the sampling train to indicate that virtually all the iodine present in the
feed was emitted as a molecular gas (I2). This observation is consistent with previous melter tests
with HLW wastes that show that iodine is retained in the glass only at 20 to 30 percent of feed if
sufficient reductants are added to the feed [10-12, 15-18]. Also in keeping with previous tests
with HLW wastes [8, 10–19], boron, sulfur, selenium, and the halides were the only elements
detected in the impinger solutions collected downstream of the heated particle filter in the
sampling train, which constitutes the “gas” fraction of the melter emissions.

5.2 Gases Monitored by FTIR and Gas Chromatography

 Melter emissions were monitored in each test for a variety of gaseous components, most
notably CO and nitrogen species, by Fourier Transform Infra-Red Spectroscopy (FTIR). The
off-gas system temperature is maintained well above 100°C beyond the sampling port
downstream of the HEPA filter to prevent analyte loss due to condensation prior to monitoring.
A summary of average concentrations monitored during each test is provided in Table 5.2. The
analytes listed in Table 5.2 are those that were thought likely to be observed during the test based
on previous work; no other species were detected in the off-gas stream by FTIR. Also included
in Table 5.2 are average hydrogen concentrations monitored by gas chromatography over an
hour immediately before or after Method 5 sampling was conducted (see Section 5.1). As
expected, concentrations were low for most monitored species due to the low concentrations of
nitrogen and organic compounds in the feed. Nitrogen oxide was the most abundant monitored
nitrogen species emitted, as has been observed in previous melter tests [8-19]. Predictably, the
concentrations of carbon dioxide and incomplete combustion byproducts, such as carbon
monoxide and ammonia increased and nitrogen oxide concentrations decreased when sugar was
added to the feed in Tests W and Z. Hydrogen emissions were detectable and significantly above
the detection limit during Test W when sugar was present in the feed. A two fold dilution of the
exhaust stream is present between the sampling locations for the Method 5-type sampling and
downstream FTIR sampling and therefore concentrations monitored by FTIR (most notably
water) are about half those listed in Table 5.1. Significant variations in emissions occurred over
the course of each test segment due in part to changes in the melt pool cold cap.

 45

 SECTION 6.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 Seven tests using different HLW feeds were conducted on the DM100-BL to determine
the effect of variations in glass properties and feed composition on processing rates and melter
conditions (off-gas characteristics, glass processing, foaming, cold cap, etc.) at constant bubbling
rate. In over seven hundred hours of testing, the property extremes of glass viscosity, electrical
conductivity, and T1%, as well as minimum and maximum concentrations of several major and
minor glass components were evaluated using glass compositions that have been tested
previously at the crucible scale. Other parameters evaluated with respect to glass processing
properties were +/-15% batching errors in the addition of GFCs to the feed, and variation in the
sources of boron and sodium used in the GFCs. Tests evaluating batching errors and GFC source
employed variations on the HLW98-86 formulation (a glass composition formulated for HLW C-
106/AY-102 waste and processed in several previous melter tests) in order to best isolate the
effect of each test variable. Steady state processing rates were achieved for each composition
without significant problems, except for tests with the most viscous and least electrically
conductive glass formulations, which required sugar additions near test mid points in order to
reduce excessive foaming that severely limited feed processing rates. The steady-state production
rates ranged between 900 to 1600 kg/m2/day, bracketed the range of steady-state production rates
(1100 to 1400 kg/m2/day) measured on other WTP HLW waste streams processed on the DM100
under similar conditions, and were higher for lower viscosity (and higher electrical conductivity)
glass compositions. The sugar additions during tests with the more viscous glass formulations
allowed a near doubling of production rates from about 500 to 900 kg/m2/day. The glass
processing difficulties caused by foaming occurred with the more viscous melts, presumably due
to the inability of gas to readily escape from the melt. Foaming did not increase with glass
manganese content and, in fact, the glass with the highest manganese concentration processed at
the highest rate of any the compositions tested, with no foaming problems. Tests processing the
HLW98-86 formulation with fifteen percent GFC batching errors and with boric acid and soda
ash replacing borax all resulted in steady-state production rates of between 1000 and
1050 kg/m2/day, indicating that these changes do not have a discernable effect on processing
rates.

 Samples from the melter feed, glass pool, and product glass were collected and analyzed
throughout testing to meet a variety of data objectives. Testing conducted on feed samples
showed that variations in additive to waste ratio and additive type has the potential to create
significantly more viscous feed. Dip samples collected from the melt pool at the end of each test
and discharge glass samples indicated that no separate phases had formed on the melt surface or
in the poured glass. Glass discharge samples were analyzed for composition and other properties
for comparison to target properties. Most compositional and physical properties (viscosity and
electrical conductivity) approximate the target for each test. Glass samples from tests with sugar
additions to the feed were analyzed for divalent iron concentration to determine its effect on
glass oxidation state; measured values from different compositions show different responses to

 46

the same level of sugar additions. A series of glass samples from the melt pool, discharge, and
melter bottom (sampling boat) from during and after a 1000 hour idling period were analyzed to
determine the extent of crystallization at glass temperatures of 950°C. Analyses of these samples
demonstrated the extent of crystallization near the melt pool floor; however, additional idling
tests with sampling boats at various locations would be useful to differentiate between the effects
of settling and enhanced crystal formation at the lower temperatures near the melter floor. Glass
product samples from tests with high concentrations of toxic metals were evaluated for chemical
durability and shown to be well within regulatory limits.

 Total particulate carry-over from the melter was 0.3 to 3.37 percent; however, results
from five of the eight samples were within the narrow range of 0.63 to 0.89 percent of feed
solids. This narrow range of solids carry-over is well within the range previously measured on
the DM100 when processing HLW simulants. The three sample results outside this range are
probably attributable to transient changes in the cold cap since the operational parameters were
kept constant during each test and the compositional variations are unlikely to result in such
large changes. Melter DFs were determined for most elements in the feed for all the tests
performed. The most volatile species were halides, sulfur, selenium, and thallium and, to a lesser
degree, boron, chromium, and arsenic. Gaseous emissions of nitrogen oxides and byproducts of
incomplete combustion, such as carbon monoxide and ammonia, were very low due to the lack
of nitrates and organic carbon in the feed. In tests with sugar added to the feed, higher
concentrations of these byproducts were observed, as also were lower concentrations of nitrogen
oxide.

 The results of these tests suggest that foaming in the melt pool, which severely limits
feed processing rates, depends mostly on the physical properties of the glass melt, increasing
with increasing viscosity. The concentration of manganese did not appear to be a significant
factor. Additions of sugar were demonstrated to be effective in recovering manageable
processing conditions; however, the effects of sugar additions on the oxidation state of the glass
varied significantly with feed composition. These results highlight the need for the development
of protocols for the a priori determination of which HLW feeds will require sugar additions and
the appropriate amounts of sugar to be added in order to control foaming and maintain
throughput without over-reduction of the melt (which could lead to molten metal formation).

 47
